On reaching Mumbai, my chest swelling with confidence. I left immediately for Ratnagiri. Baba had said to bring back 250 saplings. But how could I manage so many? I was satisfied with 50 saplings. To carry back 50 saplings was in itself an extremely difficult task. I reduced my burden by giving ten plants to each of my co-workers in Mumbai. When we boarded the train to Calcutta from Mumbai, and placed the baskets of plants on our berths, our fellow-travellers seemed surprised, even harassed. Anxiety was writ large on their faces. Some were

muttering to themselves, others were grumbling more openly. One of them said that this is a travelling compartment, not a luggage van. His complaint was not inappropriate. Even if they were our most valuable possessions, for him, they were merely plants. Finally, taking his discomfort into account, we placed all the plants near the doorway, and became free from worry.

At night when I walked towards the bathroom to relieve myself, I found that all the baskets of plants were missing. I was completely flabbergasted. I quickly woke everyone up. When reality sunk in, each one of us were beating our heads in regret. The fact remained that it was, after all, my responsibility. It was not easy to overlook the depths of Baba's displeasure. On making inquiries with the railway officials we got to know that someone had taken down all our baskets at Raipur station. I was completely dumbstruck.

Thinking about how Baba would openly beat and scold me, my heart became restless and unsettled. With an uneasy heart, I reached Kolkata. Even though I didn't have the physical burden of the plants, my brow was oppressed with the burden of sadness and defeat. It became extremely unbearable. Out of depression and fear my crooked mind thought up a plan. I bought fifty saplings from a nursery in Kolkata. Many workers would bring many different kinds of plants from their regions. My plants will merge with the rest. Who will investigate the matter?

Thinking this, I became free from worry. All the workers entrusted the plants they had brought to the Dada incharge of collecting them. The Dada incharge prepared a detailed list and in front of everyone read out to Baba the description of each of the plants brought for Him. When it was my turn, Baba said, "Arre! Look, look! The plant that Dhiishuddhananda has brought, bring it to me." I turned completely ashen. The ground beneath my feet seemed to shift, with the fear of being exposed. The Dada in charge of plants took me along. Recognizing the plant, he placed it before Baba.

As soon as He saw it, Baba became a seething mass of fury. "Ask this donkey whether this is a sapling of the alphonso mango. Does He think I'm a fool? He's trying to dupe me! Who knows where he picked these up from! Lead this shirker out of the room this moment, and tell him that if within four days he does not follow my instructions, then he will not be allowed to enter my room for the rest of his life."

I was completely unnerved by this huge punishment. In such a short time it was impossible to make another trip to Ratnagiri. I felt trapped and ashamed. When my pride had been completely broken, tears began to fall. I tried to forcibly stop my crying, and I began to choke with emotion. In my mind, I raised both my hands, I surrendered completely and asking for forgiveness, I said, "Baba! I have received sufficient punishment for my ego. I am defeated. Now You show me a way out."

A strange bell began to ring in my mind and immediately I ran to the railway station. I took along another Dada to assist me. I bought a ticket and quickly reached Raipur. At the Raipur station, I made inquiries as to whether there was a nursery close by. With address in hand I searched for the nursery. What can one say about the compassionate grace of the gracious Lord? That nursery had all our stolen plants—all the baskets were lying as we had put them together. We told the owner of the nursery that those baskets were ours. He initially refused, and even tried to deceive us.

But when we began to strongly accuse him of stealing our property and threatened to take him to the police station if he tried to outsmart us, he felt scared. "Swamiji!" he said, "Don't exacerbate the matter, just take these away." I could not believe my eyes. It felt as though I had with ease regained an invaluable treasure I had once lost. To find these lost, priceless plants in the manner I did was a task more difficult than to search out a needle in a haystack. Without doubt, not only were they lost because of Baba's will, but were also found under His cool gaze. Quickly we took the return train to Calcutta, with the plants, and having delivered this invaluable treasure to Baba, heaved a huge sigh of relief. Baba was smiling gently. At that moment I felt I had bathed from head to foot in the Ganga of His Grace and felt blessed."

O Lord of *liila*! All Your life You created all sorts of strange sport. We were fortunate that You made us the medium of Your sport. All that You wanted to bring about, if You had so wished, You could have done merely with your resolve, or with a mere gesture. Instead You got us to do Your work, through love, through scolding, through beating. O Charioteer! O Director! It was Your infinite grace that You made us the chariot and the machine of Your unearthly *liilas*. You made us to dance according to Your divine desires. At times You made us laugh,

at times cry. We sang, cried, danced, only to please You. We knew that You were omniscient, that You were omnipresent and omnipotent. Moment by moment, second by second, You would make us aware of Your omniscience, so that engrossed in our egos we would not lose our way. Finally, through Prabhat Samgiita You Yourself spread the word that You could easily make the impossible possible. If You wished You could make a flower blossom from a stone: *"Se jadi cai pashan karay phul photate pare go."*

---

> *Brahmasvarúpa brahmajna brahmabháve ca tatpara,*
> *Brahmabiijasvarúpena brahmabiija namo 'stu te.*
>
> ("O you whose true nature is Brahman, who knows Brahman, who is intent upon the ideation of Brahman, who are the *Om*--sound, the seed of the Veda, because of having the nature of *Om*--be there homage to You.")

---

# Microvita

For His disciples and devotees, Baba's supreme status, His status as Taraka Brahma, is indisputable, entirely undebatable. Simultaneously, it is reasonable to accept that for other people, this is hard to swallow. It is merely a matter of individual realization, an aspect of sentiment, a devotee's perspective, not easy to prove by argument, logic or supporting evidence.

Yes, educated, intelligent scholars of today's generation may find it easy to accept that Shrii Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar was a rare, epoch-making personality. His knowledge was immeasurable. His intelligence was potent. His scholarship was vast. As more and more of His literature is being made available to the general reader, intelligent readers and intellectual critics do not hesitate to accept His superhumanness.

There is hardly an aspect of knowledge that has remained untouched by Baba's discourses. The ideas that He put forth on every subject were completely apart from mainstream thought. On every topic he directed new vision. When experts in their field study His literature, it is not unusual for them to bow their heads before Baba's keen intelligence. They do not hesitate in the least to acknowledge the superiority of his intelligence.

The educationist and former Minister of Education for India, Dr. Pratapcandra Candra, says, "Anandamurtiji's manifestation is an extremely significant event for our country – there is absolutely no doubt about this. The thought that such a multifaceted personality came into our midst in itself creates wonder. In reality, it is a matter of great pride for us that such a splendid personality indeed came to dwell amongst us." Shrii Dhirendra Candra Mitra of Ravindra Bharati University says, "It was not my good fortune to see this great personality with my own eyes, or to even come into his contact and assimilate some knowledge and merit. Fortune kept me aloof from such privilege."

Many do not know that not a line in all His literature which has so far been published, or is in the process of being published, has issued from a pen. All the literature issued through the medium of

the spoken word. All that His disciples noted down, or tape-recorded, was later published. Whatever He spoke was written down, typed or printed during his lifetime, and then approved by Him. (Even so, only a small part of the literature was made accessible to the public during His lifetime due to lack of finances). Every sentence of the published literature is indeed original. Not a single sentence is interpolated, or is it rendered by the disciples themselves. Various kinds of analyses of His philosophy have been written by devotees; however, whatever has been published under the name of Shrii Sarkar is entirely original, and not interpolated. During His lifetime He had disallowed certain parts of His discourses to be published, knowing that until the time was right it would not be easy to digest His disputable discussions. These textual omissions have also been made with His desire and prior consent. In other words, some of His ideas are only known to a few disciples of His inner circle.

There remains perhaps no field to which Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji has not made a strong and cogent contribution. In the field of spirituality, the practical methods of *Tantra-sadhana* that He has prescribed for spiritual aspirants have impacted innumerable lives, at home and abroad. On the other hand, for the sake of philosophy He gave countless discourses on spirituality, *Tantra* and *yoga-sadhana*, which run into over a hundred books, with many more yet to be published. Within a span of merely thirty-six years He created more than two hundred books – in English, Bengali, Hindi and Sanskrit. Not only from the point of view of sheer numbers but from the point of view of the content, the variety of *bhavas* and the newness of perspectives that He brought to things, this Entity occupies a lofty position in all areas of human endeavour.

The most important of his literary works is *Anandasutram*. Divided into five chapters, supplied with a commentary by the author Himself, this tiny booklet contains the entire philosophy of Ananda Marga (spiritual, social and economic) in 85 Sanskrit sutras.

Unprecedented and unique are the new facts and new perspectives on the life and philosophy of Shrii Krishna and Sadashiva that Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji has brought to light. Through His two books, *Namah Shivaya Shantaya* and *Namami Krsnasundaram*, He paints for the reader a portrait of the all-round glory and resplendence of these two epochal entities. There is very limited literature available on Sadashiva. Baba has given a fine appraisal of this great personality's Aryan-Mongol-Dravidian-Austric following. This work contains extensive material for researchers.

With great dexterity Baba has sketched the two phases of Shrii Krishna's life – Krishna of Vraja and Parthasarathi Krishna – giving an incomparable picture of Shrii Krishna's personality. Another publication, *Krishna Tattva aur Giita Sara*, is a valuable commentary on the world-famous *Bhagavad Gita*. With reference to these extraordinary books, Shrii Ramkumar Chattopadhyaya writes, "To describe the splendour of Anandamurtiji a day would not suffice; it could take many long months. Firstly I would like to say something about two books of respected Anandamurtiji – the first is *Namah Shivaya Shantaya*. Through this book He opens up doors to many new directions. His second book is *Namami Krsnasundaram*. I found both these most wonderful. He has discovered another path for us, by not treading the beaten track."

Linguistics, the science of letters and the science of grammar were subjects very dear to Him, and the books that have been published on these areas have astounded the most erudite language experts. His command over comparative linguistics, new ways of pronunciation, comparative differences between languages and dialects, the etymology of words, and historical linguistics, were completely extraordinary, almost difficult to believe. Dr. Hardev Bahri of the Department of Hindi, University of Allahabad, has this to say: "When I read Shrii Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar (Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji's) works on linguistics and grammar I was left dumbfounded. For the last forty to fifty years I knew Shrii Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar to be a spiritual personality. He had visited Allahabad two to three times and had held Dharma Maha Cakra here as well. Because his discourses contained many references to meditation and philosophy I had thought him to be a spiritualist, someone who had a special authority over *Tantra* matters, someone who was an enlightened saint. But I did not get to know that his authority extended likewise to language, culture, literature, geography, history, and so on."

Principal Nirmal Narayan Gupta writes, "There is one more speciality of Shrii Prabhat Ranjan that is worth mentioning: he is primarily an etymologist of the various aspects of grammar. In this field he is the most suitable successor to Panini, the greatest ancient Indian grammarian. We know that language is a vast ocean. In this understanding, in order to cross the ocean, one must have a suitable boat. The ark on which Shrii Prabhat Ranjan has set us afloat is called

'etymology'. There can be no more useful and reliable boat than that." Dr. Surendra Dube, Reader, Department of Hindi, Dindayal Upadhyay Gorakhpur University, writes in his book *Varna Vijnana: Shrii Sarkar's Thoughts on Language*, "In *Varna Vijnana* it is amazing to find how accurately the author pinpoints the nature of phonemes. It is only because of his extraordinary genius that Shrii Sarkar could create this amazing work."

In order to create a beautiful, healthy '*sadvipra*' society, Baba, the great visionary, developed for humankind a modern, unprecedented system called Progressive Utilization Theory (PROUT). Within it lies the cure for every ailment of the times – social, geographic, political, economic. What is most significant is that at a time when corruption in politics, disintegrating social structures and ruined economies are the order of the day, many intelligent minds have indeed been drawn to Shrii Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar's PROUT. Dr. Pranav Kumar Chattopadhyaya, Head of the Department of Economics at Kalyani University, writes, "What attracted me greatly was the fact that Anandamurtiji elaborated on a special direction, a special dimension of economics, quite outside the sphere of the conventional economics normally studied by most of us." Dr. Zahrul Haq, Professor in the Department of Economics of Dhaka University writes, "In Anandamurtiji's opinion, it is only through the creation of block-level decentralization and the changing of the party-system created by politicians, that democracy can blossom. Anandamurtiji's conception of socio-economics has opened out a new direction in the world of economics. Hitherto, economists had not formulated any special ideas on this subject."

While Anandamurtiji was certainly one whose creed was universalism, through 'Neo-Humanism' He gave an entirely new perspective to society, a perspective which underscores the compelling need to understand the existential value of flora and fauna for sustaining environmental equilibrium. Pareshcandra Modak, a professor well-known in education circles of Bangladesh, says, "Shrii Sarkar has charted a new course with reference to Neo-Humanism. Here humanism is not for humans alone, but has been spread over the animate and inanimate worlds, where man, living beings, plants, animals, birds, insects, the sun, the moon, the planets, the constellations, the milky way, are all bound by an unbreakable bond. Keeping this sentiment alive in the mind, every moment, the root of the matter, then, is to continue to behave appropriately with the entire created world."

Baba's contribution to the field of music, song and dance is extraordinary and unprecedented. He invented a new dance for humanity, called Kaushikii, and re-established the greatness of the *tandava*, the dance that Sadashiva developed. He introduced new methods of kiirtana and spoke of its greatness. In addition, He composed 5018 songs complete with melody and rhythm, a new *gharana* (school) of music popularly known as Prabhat Samgiita. It is significant to note that all these songs in eight languages were composed in the short span of eight years alone. The melodies were also given orally. Neither did he put pen to paper, nor did he touch an instrument.

Without doubt, every one of His gifts to the world was born of spiritual wisdom, not of intellect, and hence, new: unprecedented and extraordinary by nature. Dr. Ramaranjan Mukhopadhyay, the Dean of Tirupati University, Andhra Pradesh, says that in Prabhat Samgiita, Anandamurtiji has composed songs beyond the realm of the mind. He says, "In Prabhat Samgiita there is marvellous artistry in the conjunction of rhythm, delicacy of word, and depth of world. Within the narrow confines of art, one finds reflections of the Cosmos. For this reason, the essence of Prabhat Samgiita cannot be grasped through the intellect alone."

Baba's children's literature and humorous short-stories are unequalled in their field. If His children's literature sparks the innate curiosity of a child, His stories are simple, easy and juicy. Simultaneously, He wrote some spiritual stories as well, which were, largely, autobiographical in nature. By providing para-psychological explanations for His supernatural perceptions He reveals to the reader some of the secrets of His mysterious nature.

His knowledge of agriculture and botany was incomparable and without measure. Shrii Pareshcandra Modak, Assistant Professor in the Department of Agriculture of the Bangladesh Agriculture University, in his article "Shrii Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar's Rules of Farming," writes, "On the subject of agriculture, Shrii Sarkar's multifaceted knowledge stretches out infinitely. Shrii Sarkar says, farming will have to be given as much importance as fine-art. However, upto now no directed policy has been followed in the field of agriculture." On Shrii Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar's book *Ideal Farming*, Shrii Brajabhushan Sharma, the Assistant Director of the Gorakhpur branch of Doordarshan, goes so far as to say, "*Ideal Farming* is a good book for the public to follow, and is worthy of note. It is the language of the

author, the depth of the subject, research and arguments that are all important. It should be preserved at an international level for the use of research teams."

Baba's knowledge of history knows no bounds. Numerous are the unknown facts that He disclosed to His disciples on field-walk, which we unfortunately do not have a complete record of. His book on the civilization of Rarh is incomparable. He also taught how to live a beautiful, balanced, ideal human life, as outlined in what has come to be known as 'sixteen points'. Along with this, *Caryacarya* (in three parts) formulates the individual and collective ideal social life. *Jiivan-veda* gives guidelines for living and gives a new commentary on *yama-niyama*. In the field of education His contributions are extraordinary. Not only did He provide the philosophical base for a new educational system, but during His lifetime He started over a hundred learning centers all around the world. To govern these He created a central educational body – the *gurukula*.

He made every attempt and wrote widely in order to restore to women their place in society. His book *The Awakening of Women* is a milestone on this path. Even if Baba does not see eye to eye with the feminist movement, His views on the status of women are indeed revolutionary. Dr. Narayan Candra Viswas, lecturer in the Pali and Sanskrit Department, Dhaka University, writes, "His book *The Awakening of Women* is excellent for delineating the appropriate place of women in society, and for making an assessment of their contributions. If one makes an effort to materialize all that he prescribes into reality, to create a revolution, then the status of women will certainly improve. When women begin to progress it means that men's progress is also ensured and thus the entire human society progresses. By repressing one half, the other half cannot progress. Man and woman fulfill the need for the other."

*Yogic Treatment and Natural Remedies* is another of His books that is unique in its field. It is indeed a boon for the lasting welfare of humanity. Baba had particular knowledge of medicine, especially related to natural and herbal cures. Baba had given an extremely lucid appraisal of treating ailments through yoga-postures and the nutritional value of specific foods.

It is necessary to mention here that, apart from the many Indian languages His books have been translated into, they have also been translated into many languages of the world, such as Italian, French, German, Korean, Spanish, Portuguese and so on. In short, because of extraordinary intellectual and intuitive insight, each of the books of Shrii Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar are invaluable in their worth. This is why readers all over the world are transfixed, even overwhelmed, when they come into contact with his writings.

Dr. Haripad Chakravarty, Chair, Department of Bengali, University of North Bengal, once said about Baba's multifaceted knowledge, "I saw that on a single day He would give songs, He would speak about language, and on the same day have a discussion on PROUT, just as Shiva, the five-faced one has five mouths. From one mouth the science of language, from another, discussions on dharma, from the third, discussions on society, from the next, words on Neo-Humanism, and from the last, Prabhat Samgiita."

Most importantly, He made a mysterious, but unprecedented contribution to the field of biology and psychology, which He christened with a new name, "microvita." From Baba's time itself, there were two main international annual gatherings of Margiis – one on Anandapurnima (Vaeshakhi Purnima) the other on the New Year (January 1). During Baba's lifetime they used to be called Dharma Mahacakra. Today they are called Dharma Mahasammelan. The day before the Dharma Mahacakra, Baba would give an enlightening discourse in His capacity as the Head of R. U. (Renaissance Universal – an organization He had founded for the all-round awakening of human society.) The discourse He gave on 31ˢᵗ December 1983 in Kolkata as Head of R. U. was on a completely new subject. For the entire world this was totally new information. The subject was "Microvitum: The Mysterious Emanation of the Cosmic Factor." Before this, Baba had never once mentioned this subject or this word in His earlier discourses. This was His entirely new invention, without earlier writings or speech, entirely new, entirely unprecedented.

Microvita is a mysterious emanation of the Cosmic Mind, a mysterious expression. Microvita or microbes do not only work in the biological sphere. Microbes move in the biological, the mental as well as in the spiritual domains. These microvita are more subtle than atoms, electrons, protons and neutrons. In places, its material existence is more subtle, and in others it is more crude. In the crude state they can be seen with a highly specialized microscope, but in subtle form they cannot be seen. The subtlest microvita exist in the form of waves, and it is only a spiritually developed mind that can grasp these waves with the help of mental feeling, rather than through the *tanmatras* of sound, touch, form, taste and smell of the material strata. *Yaksa*, gandharva,

*kinnara*, *vidyadhara*, *videhalina*, *prakrtilina* and *siddha*, seven types of *devayoni* described in the *Puranas*, are types of subtle microvita. These are all friendly, positive microvita.

In the living world, it is possible to carry out research in this direction in the field of physics, chemistry, biology and nuclear science, and the invention and spread of newer and newer technologies is entirely possible. These microvita may prove beneficial to human welfare in multifarious ways. The all-round welfare of human beings is possible via these microvita. They can provide inspiration to every life form that has a soul; they can encourage spiritual progress and development in the environment. If scientists were to conduct microvita research in the external world, and simultaneously spiritual sages were to conduct microvita research in the spiritual world, an unprecedented revolution would take place in the universe. There would be the much-awaited union of science and spirituality; numerous mysterious, knotty problems of the psychic and psycho-spiritual worlds would be solved.

Just like positive microvita, there are many types of inimical or negative microvita in the universe as well. These negative microvita, in the form of harmful diseases, can trouble human beings in many ways. There will come a time when medical science, through proper research, will be able to free human society from these disease-causing microvita. According to Baba's exposition, the word "virus" in medical science is an unclear, and unintelligible term. Negative microvita create physical and psychic ailments in the universe. With the daily rise in man-made pollution of the land, water and air, the arena of influence of these negative microvita increases.

In recent times, these negative microvita have begun to spread rapidly through the universe. Many known and unknown diseases such as cancer, AIDS, Parkinson's, and the like, are the result of the increasing spread of these inimical microvita. Numerous unknown, incurable diseases have their source in these microvita. So also many psychic diseases are also caused by these microvita. As long as pollution increases, the possibilities for the rise in inimical microvita will also continue to increase. It is not necessary that these inimical microvita should originate on this earth; they can also come from other planets from where they originate. Their kingdom is the collective sphere of the universe. Benevolent thoughts can counter these enemy microvita; however, because of the increase in sinfulness in the universe, they are constantly arising anew. For the sake of environmental balance, it is very necessary to rapidly spread positive microvita. In order to counter the influence of negative microvita it is necessary to prepare the protective shield of positive microvita. A brilliant example of the terrible manner in which human beings can be afflicted by the harmful, swift-spreading diseases caused by negative microvita is given us in this mysterious, fascinating incident from Baba's life. As interesting as this story is, it is also especially worth telling. In order to increase the interest, instead of retelling the story as though it took place in the past, it is told as though it were taking place in the present, before one's eyes.

Acarya Vitamohananda is the Trainer (Central Training Secretary) of the Ahmedabad training center. It is the year 1988, the last week of September. Despite the *sattvika*, clean, unpolluted environs of the training center, suddenly a strange disease takes seed. How surreptitiously the disease crept in, and wove its dangerous web! No one had a clue.

In Ahmedabad, Vitamohanandaji would frequent Dr. A. K. Shah's residence quite regularly – he was a Margii and friend, and a well-respected physician. On one of his routine visits to Dr. Shah's home one evening, the doctor was out. His good-natured wife is also a doctor. As always she asked about the well-being of Dada, about his health, about the trainees. Suddenly her trained physician's eyes fell on Dada's feet. She thought there was something unusual about them. She asked, "Dada! Today your feet seem strangely swollen. What's wrong?"

Sannyasis hardly ever bother about their bodies. Without looking down at his feet, he said, "No, Didi! There's nothing wrong. It's possible that they must be swollen due to walking about excessively in the sun. There's no real problem."

A doctor's eyes are trained however. She insists, "No, Dada! This swelling is not from excessive walking – this is something quite different." Dada again changed the subject, and himself forgot about it.

Does adversity disappear by covering one's eyes, rabbit-like, with one's ears? Does the problem go away? No, never! This problem was not one to go away. Even if one ignores something, one cannot overlook the seriousness of the matter. On returning to the center, as evening falls, his feet became heavily swollen and puffy. As his name suggests, Dada indeed has a natural disengagement with bodily matters. But even the penance of such a one came to naught when he saw that, like himself, four trainees had also developed an unusual swelling on their feet. He was now frightened. Before a diagnosis could be made,

without exception, each one of the trainees developed swollen feet. "Baba, Baba! What sort of misfortune is this?"

It was noticed that gradually, other parts of the body also began to swell. Dada's peace of mind was lost, his night's sleep disturbed. Despite the concerted efforts of doctors, both Margii and non-Margii alike, it was not possible to diagnose the problem. All possible causes were looked into, all effort made to trace research on the subject, but they got no closer to an understanding of the disease than they were when they began.

Not a single trainee at the training center remained untouched by the disease. Everyone's feet swelled up. Some even had swollen hands. The doctors searching for the cause of the disease found their own hands and feet swelling with exhaustion! Newer and newer symptoms developed, symptoms which had hitherto never been spoken of in physician's manuals. As time passed, the victims found it more and more difficult to breathe, especially by night. They lost their appetite completely. They did not feel thirsty, but there was a constant increase in urination. While defecating they felt a biting, burning sensation in the anus, as though the anal walls were being cut by a razor. When all were in the grip of disease, who was left to nurse them? What could the householder brothers and sisters do to help? How much could they help?

Each one of the well-known doctors of the town was consulted, but not one of them could diagnose the problem. At night, the suffering of the trainees would reach such unbearable heights that finally four to five of them had to be hospitalized. These patients were put on artificial respirators, through which they got some relief. Despite completing every possible test in the hospital, they were not able to prescribe appropriate medication. Once they'd received all test-reports, the doctors would be non-plussed and would not know what steps to take. No cure was found. How long could the patients be kept on pain-killers? Gradually every sort of pain-killer began to lose its efficacy.

Eight to ten days passed as though they were ten long months. Everyone was being eaten away with negative thoughts. In the midst of all this worry, as though to add fuel to fire, something devastating happened. One of the trainees started to vomit blood and finally passed away. As this took place in the hospital, an effort was made to keep this secret from the other trainees. But how could such news be kept hidden for long? Each of the trainees was now brought face-to-face with the horrific specter of death. Dada Vitamohanandaji spared no effort to boost the morale of the suffering trainees, inspiring them to surrender their all to Baba. But how long could this inspiration sustain them? He himself was suffering tremendously, and completely harrassed with pain. Gradually his own condition began to worsen. He lost confidence in his own self. The little dam of moral strength he had held in place was crumbling. Even when one takes Baba's name every moment, it is hard to sustain courage.

Around this time, as per the rules, an examiner arrived from the Central Training Center to test the trainees. His name was Sadhaneshvaranandaji. When he got to know of the grave situation in which the trainees were, he was completely taken aback. He asked the local trainer why this matter had not yet been brought to Baba's notice.

Vitamohanandaji replied that in the initial phase they did not wish to disturb Baba with their personal problems. But when the situation began to worsen they did send news to Kolkata via Dr. Shah. "What message did you receive?" The examiner Dada was curious.

"None at all" was the reply. Now he was totally non-plussed. "How can this be?" He lost his cool. That very night he personally contacted the Center by telephone. At the other end of the line was G.S. Dada.

Sadhaneshvaranandaji: "G.S. Dada–it's me, Sadhaneshvarananda, calling from Ahmedabad. Here the condition of the trainer and the trainees is extremely serious. What are Baba's orders?"

G.S. Da: "Actually, the thing is that Baba is very busy these days. Also he is dissatisfied with workers not fulfilling their targets even after the deadlines have past. Baba is very upset and angry about this. Because of his mood I have not been able to tell him anything about this personally." Saying this G.S. Da tried to shake off responsibility.

Sadhaneshvarananda, expressing his difference of opinion: "But G.S. Da – it's a life-and-death issue concerning so many people. The situation is very grave. It is important to inform Baba, even if it makes His mood worse, even if it adds to His worries." And finally releasing the most effective missile in his arsenal: "G.S. Da! Why don't you realize that if we don't inform Baba about this, and if we delay further, what if he gets upset for not telling Him earlier – then what reply will we be able to give Him?"

This time the examiner dada's argument worked. G.S. Da Sarvatmanandaji was startled, "Yes, what you say is right. But now it is 11:30 at night. Tomorrow, as soon as it is possible, I will definitely

inform Him." The examiner Dada is now satisfied with G.S. Da's reply and closes his eyes to rest his worried brain for a while.

Now let us go to Kolkata. G.S. Da can barely sleep through the night. As dawn breaks, having finished his morning routine, he walks towards Baba's room. After a while, Baba opens His door in readiness for His morning field-walk. Without delay, in one long breath, G.S. Da tells Baba all that has happened, and then heaves a sigh of relief.

It has been seen many times in the past that even when Baba was given the worst possible kind of news, He would remain unshaken, steady. There would be no upset expression on His lotus-face. But this time, when Baba was given the news, once could clearly discern anxiety disturb His face. He changed His mind about going on field-walk. This would rarely ever happen. He sat down in the neighbouring room. For a while He sat, with eyes closed, deep in thought. G.S. Da and the other workers sat around, frightened. After a one-minute silence, Baba said, "This is a fatal disease, a negative microvita that has come to the earth from another planet. This is such a terrible and fearsome disease that if it is not destroyed at its very root, it could wipe out the entire human race."

All the workers present there were stunned by fear. Like helpless children they stared at Baba's face. They were thinking, "Won't Baba protect human society?" Their gazes were mixed with a blend of curiosity and anxiety.

After a while Baba revealed that, had this fatal disease not struck the Ahmedabad training center, but travelled elsewhere, none would have been able to control the complete destruction of humankind. It seemed as though Baba was saying that a major catastrophe was limited by Him. For the sake of protecting humankind He had controlled the spread of this negative microvita by limiting it to the boundaries of the training center, so that even if His own sons, who have dedicated their lives for His work, go through unbearable suffering, and even death, at least human society is saved.

In a grave mood, Baba asks, "Do you have all names of the trainees?" G.S. Da was well-prepared. He immediately responded, "Yes, Baba – we have them." Baba then said, "Go! In the name of each trainee pick a red flower from the garden and bring it here immediately." Baba's instructions are quickly followed. In minutes the red flowers are brought before Him.

Baba immediately took the basket of red flowers in His hand and returned to His room. He bolted the door from within. Those sitting outside could clearly hear Baba pronouncing mantras, as though He were transmitting, through the medium of the mantra, *shakti* (cosmic energy) into the flowers. As soon as the mantric-rite was over Baba rang the bell. All the dadas, fired by curiosity, rushed in. Baba was sitting on the bed. He said, "Send one person immediately to Ahmedabad by flight. Immediately send news to Ahmedabad that all the trainees must leave the city as soon as possible. Make immediate arrangements to transfer the training center elsewhere."

Baba went on, "Take a decision, at this very moment, to transfer the training center." No one could figure out what should be done. Baba would pronounce an order as easily as it was to swallow a morsel of food. How could they probably make such enormous arrangements on the spur of the moment? Where did the Marga possess any extra land? One Dada suggested the Himmatnagar school. Baba said, "No, no – it's too small." Everyone stood around helplessly, without a solution in sight. Then Baba said, "I donate my M. G. Quarters in Gandhidham for the purposes of a training center. Transfer the training center there immediately."

All the workers present heaved a sigh of relief. Baba went on, "Make all arrangements to transfer the training center to Gandhidham, without any delay." Baba then emphasized, "By transfer I mean only the trainer and the trainees will go to Gandhidham. No clothes, no rations, no bed-clothes, no furniture, nothing at all. In Gandhidham entirely new materials will be bought and all the materials in Ahmedabad will be burnt there at the training center itself. Before leaving the center every belonging should be completely burnt to ashes. The trainees will not even carry a handkerchief in their pockets from the center. On their bodies, only minimal clothing. All food materials will also be burnt. Not a grain of food, not a drop of water should leave the place. The trainees and the trainer will be transferred together. They should not travel by train or public bus. Make arrangements for a private bus. They will travel by night, not by day. When they arrive at the new center they will bathe outside the house before entering. They will remove every shred of clothing outside the house, and then having worn a new set of clothes, will enter the house. All the clothes that they remove from their bodies will be burnt outside the house. They will be thoroughly burnt to ashes." In one breath Baba pronounced all the instructions, and had them simultaneously noted down.

Baba's emphasis on the minutest detail, placing so much stress on every instruction, was hitherto never seen before. Baba then gave

a list of all the things that each patient would eat and drink, what he should not eat or drink, and had it all noted down. Nothing should detract from the success of the patients' recovery. Baba ordered that none other than the G.T.S. (the chief trainer), Nigamananda, should himself go to Ahmedabad. It was not necessary to take the basket of flowers to Ahmedabad. Another Dada would take these directly to Gandhidham. Vandananandaji was given this responsibility.

Both of them left on the first flight to Mumbai, and thence to their individual destinations. According to Baba's own cosmic arrangements, enroute from Mumbai to Ahmedabad, Nigamanandaji made acquaintance with a person who himself volunteered to make arrangements for a private bus.

Nigamanandaji is one of Baba's especially industrious workers. Because of the gravity of the circumstances, he transcribed every one of Baba's smallest instructions into his heart and mind, and took a decision to follow them down to the last letter. As soon as he arrived he started the work of incineration. A training center has much goods and furniture. It was all piled together and then burnt. The fire went on till late into the night. Everything – except for a vest and a *lungi* on each of the trainees and their trainer – everything else was burnt to ashes.

Everyone then got into the bus arranged by Dada's co-traveller and left the place. They first stopped at the hospital, where five to six dadas had been admitted. They were in the intensive care unit on artificial respirators. Nigamanandaji entered the hospital and requested for them to be discharged. This created a hue and cry in the hospital. The doctors were completely unwilling to discharge the patients in this condition. In their opinion if the patients were removed from the respirators it would result in their instantaneous death. But in order to translate Baba's orders into reality, Nigamanandaji was not prepared to make the smallest deviation. At the behest of the Hospital Superintendent, Nigamanandaji submitted in writing, without the least hesitation, that he was solely responsible for anything that would happen to the patients once they were discharged. Dada clarified that while he did not have a problem in taking upon himself the responsibility of these patients, the hospital had also not done anything to improve their condition.

Look at the beauty of Baba's miracles! Having removed the oxygen tubes from their mouths, these patients teetering on the borders of death were then led into the bus, in which they comfortably rode to Gandhidham without any complaints. These trainees and their trainer, who had unknowingly taken upon themselves the protection of humanity through the bodily hardships they had to endure, then entered their new training center, by Baba's endless flow of grace. This in itself is yet another glowing historic example of Baba's miraculous *liilas*.

How compassionate Baba protected vulnerable humankind with the shield of his protection writes a golden page in the history of humanity. It was His own plan to limit the spread of this negative microvita from another planet, in the form of this disease, to the bounds of the training center, and keep the rest of the world away from the clutches of this adversity. It is said that this infectious disease started through the medium of water. But the trainees used water from the neighbourhood handpump from which others also drew their water. How did the disease infect only the trainees? Other than Baba's *liila* can there be any other answer to this question?

Rising above these delicate sentiments, let me direct the reader back into the story's flow, back to Gandhidham, the site of all the action. Vandanananda had made all the requisite preparations in Baba's house. Before the first rays of the morning sun could burst forth, the bus reached Baba's house in Gandhidham. Just outside the campus, there were all facilities for bathing. Each trainee bathed and left his used clothes outside the compound. For each one there was a new set of clothes ready. Before they entered they were given these. Having worn their new clothes, taking their *guru* mantra, each trainee entered the house. All the clothes that had been worn on the way from Ahmedabad were incinerated. It seemed as though that dreadful killer disease was being burnt on a funeral pyre.

On entering, each newly arrived trainee received a red flower sanctified by mantras inspired by Baba's compassion. According to Baba's instructions, each trainee touched the flower first to his knees and then to all the joints of his body, and lastly to all the cakras from *muladhara* to *sahasrara*. Finally the flower was put into a plastic bag. It seemed as though through the mantric flower Baba was making each diseased trainee whole again, making each one healthy again.

It was Baba's strict instruction that the flowers which had touched the cakras of the trainees were to be collected and sealed in a plastic bag and then thrown into flowing water. The last traces of that negative microvita should not come into contact with any other living being – neither with an animal or a plant. Once each one had gone through the process, Vandananandaji himself took the sealed bag to the Kandla

port, and threw it deep into the sea. What we are left with is great awe in the face of this strange incident, this unprecedented miracle! From that day onwards, from that moment, all the patients gradually become free of the disease. Those patients who were on their deathbed, who had been admitted to the hospital and were surviving on respirators, even they were regaining their health. Is there indeed a limit to the *liilas* of the Lord?

The central secretary made a list of dos and don'ts for each trainee according to what Baba had prescribed for each. They were not allowed to eat any root, nor were they allowed any milk. Yogurt-water could be had. Fresh tomatoes were to be boiled with their skins. Their skins were then to be removed and the tomatoes pureed and made into a shorba. This was to be fed morning and evening to the trainees with a little black pepper. They were not supposed to have anything other than this for breakfast. Light meals at night were recommended. Those areas which were swollen had to be bound with leaves of the *kadamba* tree by night. Since there were no *kadamba* trees in Gandhidham, *kadamba* leaves were being couriered to Gandhidham for a few days. The swelling continued for seven to eight days and finally the pain receded completely, leaving no trace of swelling.

Except the trainer dada: for a long while after the cure of the disease, he had several remnant aches and pains. His swellings also remained for a long while, and he would feel pain when he would try to twist his body to the left or right. There was a slight pain in his backbone. Perhaps he still had some *samskaras* to be quelled. After a long while, during a reporting session, Baba suddenly asked, "Where is Vitamohananda? Send for him."

It was the 15th of October 1990. By phone, Vitamohanandaji was asked to come to Kolkata from Gandhidham. On 20th October he was made to stand up during the reporting session. Baba let His gaze fall over his body, from head to foot, then asked, "Tell me, where do you feel pain? What kinds of afflictions do you have?" One by one, Dada mentioned each one of his problems. Baba asked him to turn around, with his back towards Him. Dada does as instructed. Baba makes a cross sign with His pain-dispelling stick, on a special point on his back, then presses down firmly with it. He then asks, "Vitamohananda—now tell me, how is your pain?" He twisted his body from left to right, up and down, he moved in every direction, bending forwards and backwards. He could not believe it. Again he twisted and turned. How strange, all the pain had evaporated!

This incident took place exactly a day before Baba's *Mahaprayana*. Till the very last moment before leaving the body of five fundamental factors that He had taken on by His will, that bestower of grace was concerned for the pain and sadness of his children. How compassionate is He! Which child would not count himself blessed for having found such a compassionate Baba? It was either the fruit of good deeds of countless lives, or else merely His causeless grace that we could come so close to Him, that we could play with Him in many ways, could run, jump and dance around Him. Alas! What wonderful days were those! Sweet memories of those paradisial days remain the only support of life. By His grace, by His inspiration, Hamrahi has taken upon himself to spread the delicious flavour of those days to one and all, so that the relish of those days should not be limited to those who experienced them. Oh Bestower of Compassion! Oh Repository of Grace! Grace Hamrahi so that till his dying breath, his pen may have strength and ability, that till his last breath he will continue to sing your praise and waft the fragrance of your sweetness on the air.

> *Yeshám samskaranát pumsám sadyah shudhyanti vai grháh,*
> *Kim punar darshanasparshapádashaocásanádibhih.*
>
> ("There are people the mere thought of whom purifies one's home instantly;
> how much more so by seeing them, touching them, washing their feet, and offering them a seat?")

## The Lord's Insatiable Hunger for Sweet and Sanctified Devotion

The outstanding feature of Baba's mission lies in the total dedication of each one of its workers towards His cause. Indeed, they are totally and thoroughly committed to His mission. Baba's aim is to elevate *dharma* and re-establish its rule in society. For Him, *dharma* does not imply any particular religion, sect or doctrine. By seeking to re-establish *dharma*, He aims to revive Mánava Dharma, Bhágavata Dharma or Sanátana Dharma. This singular ideal is the driving force behind His mission.

Certainly one needs missionaries for the establishment of such a mission, and the workers of the Marga fulfill this role. There are whole-timers and part-time workers and they comprise of both *sannyasiis* and the house-holders. However, it is the full-time *sannyasii* workers who play the leading role in the functioning of the mission. Innumerable youngsters were impressed by Baba's omniscient nature. They were also swayed and influenced by His divine occult powers. Above all they could not resist the charm of His deeply affectionate and attractive personality. Attracted by His magnetic personality, many youngsters were inspired to dedicate their lives in the service of His mission.

Do you think this can be dismissed as an ordinary and insignificant sacrifice? In this day and age the majority of the youth are lured by the decadent prevalent culture. Enticed by its glamour, they chase after the world of material comforts. They seem to be drawn towards it as inexorably as the glow-worm seeks the flame or the river flows towards the ocean. Inspite of this un-congenial environment, some youngsters still preferred to lead a *dharmic* life. They consciously chose to associate themselves with Baba's mission and dedicated their lives for its cause. Surely this is no trifling matter! Their self-sacrifice and dedication, the implicit faith in their ideal and in the Preceptor, are all part of the most resplendent chapter in the history of our age.

It was customary for Baba to transfer the whole-time workers from one post to another or from one field of work to another, or even from one region to another. The worker never aspired for any post or position, nor was he intimidated by the difficulties he would have to face in a new and unknown area. Like the proverbial ox, the worker too would put his or her shoulder to the yoke and faithfully carry out the duties and responsibilities associated with the new assignment. The mental attitude of the workers could be likened to the disregard and detachment with which a snake tosses off its slough. This was the outstanding feature of their character and temperament. Therein lay their greatness and excellence.

In particular, the dedication and sacrifice of the workers in the early years of the mission were immeasurable and deserve a special mention. In those early days there was negligible *pracara*, and contact with people was also limited. Ever so often, many of the Dadas would have nothing to eat and had to live and cope with constant hunger. Later on too, workers who were posted in new and strange places had to contend with many problems and difficulties.

The following is the story of one such dedicated worker who had been posted to the north-eastern region of India. This area lacked in basic amenities and was therefore unpopular with the workers. Any worker posted there had to struggle very hard indeed. Baba's aim was to lead mankind towards a predetermined goal. Furthermore, this had to be achieved within a limited time-span. This was the reason behind the extraordinary speed and urgency in His style of working. Every worker was handed out an extraordinarily long and extensive list of tasks and projects to be completed. These plans and projects would invariably be very difficult to accomplish and some targets would be impossible to achieve. Over and above these duties, the worker also had to contend with local problems in his field.

All these factors served to aggravate an already difficult situation, and it is hard to imagine the tension and pressures under which the workers had to function. In due course, the workers learnt to cope with any situation, and adapted themselves to every eventuality. This ability to blend harmoniously with the prevailing circumstance, together with their dedication towards the mission, generated in them tremendous courage and confidence. They had to run the school and make it "healthy." Simultaneously they had to organize *pracara* work and successfully execute various plans and projects that Baba would assign them.

The particular worker referred to in our story would spend five days in the week managing the affairs of a school. Saturdays and Sundays were reserved for *pracara* work. Every Saturday, he would leave at six in the morning, exploring a new field, and would return late at night on Sunday. Preoccupied with his work, he would forget about his meals. Thoughts of food and drink seldom entered his mind. One Saturday, as he was about to leave for his field at dawn, he happened to look into the kitchen. He realized that he had run out of provisions. He decided to leave for the field and planned to make his purchases of rice, dal, vegetables, oil, salt and spices on his way back on Sunday night.

It so happened that by the time he returned on Sunday, it was already late into the night. Besides, he had been so involved in his work that he had totally forgotten about the purchases he had planned to make. It was ten o'clock in the night and the shops had already closed. Moreover, two days of toil in the field had left him totally exhausted. Overcome with fatigue he could not think of stepping out again.

He inspected all the tins and containers to check for any remains. Luckily, he found just a handful of rice and dal, but there was neither salt nor turmeric left. All the same he decided to make a '*khichdi*', even without salt and turmeric. After placing the rice and dal to cook on the stove, he washed his hands and feet and sat down for *sadhana*. However, he was so exhausted that he soon dozed off. A while later he was startled out of his sleep. Something was burning and he dashed to the kitchen. The *khichdi* was burnt. The worker shook his head in dismay and exclaimed, "Oh Baba! How did this happen? How unfortunate that even this meager food was not in my lot!" He tried to salvage what remained of the burnt *khichdi*. He scraped the surface and placed the half-scorched rice onto a plate, did guru *puja* and sat down to eat. But it was hardly a meal. Just a few meager morsels which he washed down with a glass of water.

Several months after this incident, the Dada arrived in Kolkata for his usual reporting session. This episode took place approximately a year prior to our most compassionate Father's *Mahaprayana*. By that time, several changes had been incorporated in the reporting system that Baba had initiated. Baba had been strictly warned by the doctors to avoid undue fatigue and exhaustion, as His body could not stand up to it. The global workers too tried as much as they could to persuade Baba to avoid physical stress and strain. As a result, all the organizational reporting was being conducted by the General Secretary, (G.S. Dada), in the presence of Baba.

Whatever scolding or punishment was to be meted out was done so by G.S. Dada himself. He would turn to Baba, look at Him intently, and continue to rebuke the concerned worker. Baba would not say a word but would carefully listen to the entire proceedings as a witnessing entity. Very rarely would He interrupt or interfere in the proceedings. This was the regular practice adopted for all the reporting sessions, irrespective of whether it was a Bhukti Pradhan session, a W.T. session or an L.F.T. reporting session. The same procedure was adhered to in the case of all levels and departments, whether regional, sectorial or global.

In order to assess whether a worker had completed all the tasks assigned to him, G.S. Dada would scrutinize the relevant "Work Done Report." This would give him a clear indication of the amount of work done by the particular worker. When Baba Himself used to conduct the reporting sessions, He would simultaneously deal with two aspects of the worker's performance. He would certainly examine the "Work Done Report" and at the same time He would also conduct the worker's individual "*dharma samiiksha*." In the course of this appraisal, Baba would look into any personal shortcomings of the worker. He would also point out any flaws in individual conduct and behaviour, with particular stress on the adherence to Conduct Rules and Sixteen Points. Finally, Baba would minutely scrutinize the physical health of the worker and immediately lay bare with X-Ray-like clarity the exact details of the worker's health problem.

All this was well beyond the scope of G. S. Dada. That is why Baba introduced a system by which G.S. Dada would select any one worker from each group, and Baba would then personally conduct his examination. Baba would give maximum time to the selected representative worker, so that his entire personal examination could be completed in one session. This individual examination would at times last for ten to fifteen minutes or at times it would go on for hours at a stretch. No one would interrupt or intervene during such proceedings.

G.S. Dada would usually try his best to give this opportunity to a worker who may have come from a great distance, especially one who had never had such a chance before. At times it would so happen that Baba Himself would call out the name of a worker and ask him to come forward.

At the time of this episode, the I.R.S.S. of the Kolkata region was in progress. Hence a large number of workers from this region had assembled there. Included amongst these workers was the diocese level worker of the north-eastern region. G. S. Dada selected this very worker who was holding the post of D.S. from the north-eastern diocese. He is the same worker mentioned in the earlier part of this chapter. Baba instructed G.S. Dada to start by taking a detailed report of the work done by the said worker. Accordingly, G.S. Dada began the reporting session.

In a grave and serious tone G.S. Dada questioned the worker, "How is your school functioning?"

"It is alright," came the D.S.'s vague reply.

Not pleased with the reply, G.S. Dada continued more sternly:

"How many children are there?"

The worker could not give a satisfactory reply. G.S. Dada continued, "Your school is not healthy. What efforts have you made to improve the situation?"

The worker made no reply.

"Alright, how many new admissions have been made in the last one month?," asked G.S. Dada, as he continued with the questioning.

"Two new children," replied the worker hesitatingly.

"Do you think this is a satisfactory state of affairs?," asked the G.S. gravely.

Even as the G.S. proceeded with his examination, he would constantly turn towards Baba, lest He may pronounce some punishment for the G.S. to implement. However, Baba showed no reaction. G.S. Dada continued with his inquiry. One by one he took up all the issues and features associated with the reporting session. He questioned the worker regarding pracara work, the state of the home, and that of the medical unit he was required to start and manage. The report was far from satisfactory. The worker had not fulfilled his responsibilities in any one of the manifold areas of his work.

Whenever Baba would hear such an unsatisfactory report, He would fly into a rage and severely punish the worker concerned. He would even take a solemn assurance from the worker that in future he would fulfill all his duties and responsibilities. But on this particular day, Baba showed no reaction at all. He continued to recline against the bolster, His head supported by His right palm, eyes lowered. His face betrayed no expression at all. Several times in the course of the reporting session, G.S. Dada would pause and turn towards Baba, anxiously awaiting His reaction. But on that day there was not even a trace of a reaction from Baba. Finally, at the end of the reporting session, after the G.S. had finished with his questionings, Baba turned to the worker. He looked at him intently, His eyes roving over him from top to toe.

Then Baba turned towards the G.S. and said, "G.S.! This worker has made one grave mistake. He has not fulfilled his duties and responsibilities."

G.S. Dada thought to himself that it was quite obvious, from the reporting just conducted in Baba's presence, that the worker had not completed even one of his various duties and assignments. But which particular serious mistake was Baba referring to? How was the G.S. to know?

Baba continued further: "Ask him as to what mistake he has committed, and whether he is willing to admit his fault."

Parrot-like, the G.S. repeated Baba's questions to the worker: "Tell us, what mistake have you committed? And are you willing to admit your fault?"

The poor worker was confused, unable to fathom what was implied. In his helplessness, he stood there silent, unable to speak a word. Baba went on to say: "He should certainly be punished for the mistake he has committed. Tell me G.S., should he be punished or not?"

Usually Baba would express much displeasure when a worker would be at fault. His facial expression would change, and at times He would assume a look that could be dreadfully fearsome. But on this occasion Baba's reaction was very surprising indeed. While He continued to lay stress on the gravity of the mistake, there was not a crease on His brow. In the course of the regular daily sessions, G.S. Dada had studied Baba's facial expressions and could read them quite competently. Indeed, he had become an expert at comprehending Baba's reactions and emotions. But on that particular day even he was quite amazed.

Still trying to fathom Baba's intentions, he nevertheless voiced his agreement and replied, "Yes Baba! Certainly he must be punished."

To this Baba said, "Ask him whether he deserves to be punished," The worker was still in the dark, but without further hesitation he admitted his fault and was ready to accept punishment. The moment the worker admitted his mistake, Baba's tone of voice changed

radically. Initially Baba's stand was that of a prosecutor. Now He began to speak on behalf of the defendant, as an advocate for the accused!

Baba said, "This boy is extremely hardworking and is absolutely honest and trustworthy. He is not a shirker and is certainly not a fool. Besides, he whole-heartedly tries to fulfill all his duties and responsibilities." On hearing this powerful argument in favour of the defendant, G.S. Dada at once understood that the worker's fault had nothing to do with any organizational lapse. Rather, it was something related to his personal conduct and behaviour. Surely he must have seriously violated either the rules of *yama* and *niyama* or those of the Sixteen Points.

G.S. Dada thought that Baba was now about to reveal the worker's mistake in the midst of this assembly, and would have him punished for the same. But to his further surprise and amazement, Baba went on to praise even his personal conduct and behaviour. He said, "He strictly follows the rules of *yama* and *niyama* and those of the Sixteen Points."

By now everyone present there was thrown into utter confusion. In that case what grave mistake could he have committed? Adding further to their confusion, Baba asked the G.S., "G.S.! Ask him as to why on that particular night, he committed a mistake which otherwise he would never make?" Which was that night and what was that mistake? Neither the G.S. nor anyone else present there could fathom anything. Even the worker concerned continued to remain in the dark. Thereupon Baba went on to reveal the entire episode, already mentioned above, to all the assembled workers. He revealed how the worker had left at dawn on that particular Saturday for *pracara* work.

Relating further, Baba recalled how the worker had returned home late the following Sunday night. There were no rations in the home and so the worker had hastily prepared a *khichdi* from the handful of rice and *dal* that had remained. He had placed this *khichdi*, which had neither salt nor turmeric, on the stove to cook. Baba continued to relate the circumstances under which the khichdi was scorched on the stove. As He recapitulated the events concerning the episode, Baba continued to speak well of the worker. He gave no hint of the mistake the worker had committed. Once more the worker and those present were utterly confused. Finally, Baba went on to reveal how this boy, whenever he would sit down for his meals, would mentally offer the same to Baba, and only then would he partake of it. Baba stressed that this was his regular daily practice, one which he adhered to without fail.

However, Baba went on to reveal that on that particular night, He had waited, expecting to be served. However, the worker had failed to offer Him even a morsel, and so He had remained there, hungry and unfed. Was this not a punishable offence?

The accused D.S., trying to defend his action, quickly replied, "Baba! On that particular day how could I have offered You that half-burnt, unpalatable khichdi, which had neither oil nor salt nor turmeric in it?"

Baba interrupted him sharply and said, "Do you think I am pleased if someone offers me *rasagolla*? No, never. I am hungry for devotion. If anyone serves me anything with devotion I happily accept it and partake of it. It matters little if it is plain and simple fare, tasteless or tasty, cooked or uncooked, or even burnt or half-burnt. That day you consumed everything, but failed to feed me anything. I was forced to go to bed hungry and unfed."

The worker was overcome with emotion. He was unable to check the tears that flowed like a flood from his eyes. Unable to control himself, he fell headlong at Baba's feet and sobbed as he begged for forgiveness. Baba consoled him and said, "No, no, the mistake you committed that day was unintentional. Perhaps you thought that Baba would not accept such tasteless and half-burnt food. But the truth is that Baba only craves for the sweet devotional sentiment of the heart. Whatever is offered to Him with love, satisfies His hunger. But He will not touch even the most elaborate meal if it is offered without love and devotion. Whatever is offered to Him with the sweetness of one's heart is very dear to Him." Having said this, Baba blessed the prostrate worker and raised him to his feet. He patted his cheeks and showered him with much love and affection.

My Lord! This episode of Yours brings to my mind the story of Vidurani and the banana skins. It also brings to mind the anecdote in *Ramakatha* regarding Shabari and the plums she had tasted and set aside. It also reminds me of Sudama and his tiny bundle of rice.

In the *Ramakatha*, there is the story of Shabari, an ardent devotee of Ram. She is feeding her Lord with plums which she carefully tastes herself before offering them to Him. Laxman is upset by this and rebukes the tribal woman. Thereupon Ram intervenes and says, "My dear brother! These plums offered by the Bhil woman may seem impure, but they are sweet indeed. You would not find such sweetness anywhere

else! Look into her mind and heart and observe the sweet and tender feelings of selfless devotion. In her eagerness to feed her beloved she wants to make sure that she does not offer Him a single sour fruit, not even by mistake. Such wonderful sentiments these are, drenched with sweetness and love! It is these sweet sentiments and not the 'impure' fruits that I am devouring with relish."

Likewise, when Krishna's poor and humble friend Sudama goes to Dwarka to meet his childhood companion, he carries a gift for Him. As per their means, Sudama's wife wraps some pounded and roasted rice and bundles it in an old, tattered cloth. She hands over the parcel to Sudama and says: "Since you are going to visit your friend you must carry some gift with you."

When Sudama beholds the pomp and splendour of the royal court of Krishna, the King of Dwarka, he is stunned and taken aback. Overcome with awe, he cannot bring himself to give the humble and insignificant gift to Krishna. Thereupon Krishna Himself snatches the bundle that Sudama had concealed under his armpit and says, "Will you not give me the special gift sent by my sister-in-law?" Krishna's gesture sends Sudama into ecstasy and he remains speechless with emotion.

Likewise, in another instance, Krishna pays an unexpected visit to the house of the learned Vidura, only to find that he is not at home. His wife however is taken aback when she sees Krishna at the door. Absorbed in her love and devotion for the Lord, she looks around and hastens to extend a warm welcome to Krishna. In her eagerness, she brings some bananas to offer to Him. With her gaze fixed on Krishna, she begins to feed Him with the bananas.

But is she really feeding Him the fruit? Lost in her state of ecstatic devotion, she unwittingly discards the fruit and continues to offer the outer skins to Krishna. The Lord too, enchanted by the Vidurani's overwhelming display of love and devotion, continues to devour the skins. Just then, Vidura happens to return home and is shocked by the sight that meets his eyes. He beckons his wife to come to her senses and realize her mistake. Krishna then turns to Vidura and says, "Vidura, I only crave for the sanctified devotion of the human heart. The love with which your wife has been feeding me with these skins is for me nectar indeed!"

Infinite indeed is the Lord's liila and equally amazing is His longing for a devotee's sanctified love. These factors remain constant through all ages. For, is it not an irrevocable law of history that it always repeats itself?

On another occasion Baba had said, "You know, whenever my devotee, my *sadhaka* or my *sadhika* sits in *sadhana* at his or her predetermined time, I unfailingly go and sit by him or her. However, when on some days I find that he or she has not sat down for *sadhana*, then I return without having been able to meet with them."

How shameful it is that our Father, our Supreme Father invariably goes to meet with His son or daughter at that appointed time, but does not find us. Why? Because we have no time for Him! In the same way the Lord, who ever craves our devotion, waits expectantly at the appointed time to be lovingly served at mealtime by His son or daughter. But when nothing is offered to Him, He is forced to retire for the day, unfed and hungry. My Lord, how gracious You are and how selfish are we! Where can we, Your foolish and ignorant children, find refuge except at your feet and in Your gracious lap? Infinitely graceful as You are, You mercifully overlook and disregard all our childish errors. Were You not to do this from time to time, we, hapless children, would have to bear the burden of our countless sins and mistakes for time immemorial. Save us, Oh Lord! I beseech You, have mercy on us!

> *Patraṁ puṣpaṁ phalaṁ toyaṁ yo me bhaktyā prayacchati,*
> *Tad ahaṁ bhaktyupahṛtam aśnāti prayatātmanaḥ.*
>
> ("If one offers me a leaf, a flower, fruit, or water with love, I accept it, it being given with devotion from one pure at heart.")

# Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

Baba has left innumerable legacies for the benefit of mankind and the foremost amongst these is PROUT, or The Progressive Utilization Theory. It is an unsurpassable theory, based on humanitarian socio-economic principles. Its all-encompassing sweep, its indisputable integrity and unassailable principles have stunned and silenced the intellectual world. Our society, constrained as it is by vested interests and circumstantial pressures, may be hesitant in translating this theory into practice. However, the fact remains that PROUT is the one and only panacea for all the multifarious problems of this world.

Capitalism and communism, the two main branches of political and economic theory, strip the common man of his inner spirit. He is forced to live a mechanical existence. On the other hand, PROUT extols the path of human fulfillment and perfection. While embracing the path of spirituality, PROUT also grapples with the fundamental problems of human existence. It addresses the five basic human needs of food, clothing, shelter, health care and education and presents radical and pragmatic solutions for fulfilling these needs.

The five fundamental principles of PROUT are as follows:

1. No individual should be allowed to accumulate any physical wealth without the clear permission or approval of the collective body.
2. There should be maximum utilization and rational distribution of mundane, supra-mundane and spiritual potentialities of this universe.
3. There should be maximum utilization of physical, metaphysical and spiritual potentialities of the unit and collective body of human society.
4. There should be proper adjustment amongst the physical, metaphysical, mundane, supra-mundane and spiritual utilizations.
5. The method of utilization should vary in accordance with the changes in time, place and person and the utilization should be of a progressive nature.

The five principles of PROUT enumerated above comprise the most foolproof prescription for the eradication of all the socio-economic ills of our age. These principles are based on the sound foundation of *dharma* and spirituality. The critical evaluation and appraisal of these principles have already been done at length elsewhere, and hence is not dealt with in this chapter.

Communism looks upon religion as the opium of the masses. PROUT considers spirituality and *dharma* as the primary need of mankind. These are mutually conflicting views. There is no common meeting ground between the exponents of communism and those of PROUT. In fact, there exists a traditionally bitter rivalry between the two. They are poles apart, even as fire is to water, and their enmity is as fierce as that of the snake and the mongoose. This mutual contempt and seething enmity is at its fore in the political environment of Bengal. There exists a long-standing hostility between the communist government of Bengal and the Proutists. This is the underlying cause of the frequent incidents of violence involving the two parties.

The horrifying and gruesome massacre that took place at Bijan Setu will go down as one of the most hair-raising and sensational acts of carnage in the history of mankind. On that fateful day, seventeen innocent monks and nuns of Ananda Marga were brutally murdered and burnt alive. This gory incident took place in broad daylight in a crowded market square. The state government turned a deaf ear to pleas for an inquiry into this heinous crime, and in fact sheltered the criminals.

Communist party members, bearing allegiance to the red flag, bore yet another grudge against the Proutists. The reason was that many senior and responsible party members, having been impressed by Proutist principles, left the Communist Party. They had chosen to discard their red robes for the saffron of the Proutist forum. Today there are many such persons, both amongst the *sanyasiis* and the active householder Margiis, who were once staunch and active members of the Marxist Party. It would not be proper to disclose their names and identities in the present context. However, the subject of the following story was one such person, whose name we cannot withhold. His name is Maheshwar Pandey and the story that follows relates his experience.

Maheshwar Pandey hailed from Patna. He was a staunch Marxist with an unquestioning, almost blind faith in its principles. In those days of the "cold war" he was one of those Marxist Indians who would step into the street with an umbrella over his head if it happened to rain in Peking or Moscow. The mere mention of a cold wave in Shanghai or Leningrad would send shivers down his spine, in faraway India.

(This is one of the very common and popular satirical jibes that are directed against the Communist Party members in India. Their fanaticism and unquestioning acceptance of the red party political culture has inevitably invited much ridicule.)

By a stroke of good luck, Maheshwar Pandey happened to come in contact with the Marga. He undertook a thorough study of the lucid PROUT philosophy and drew his own conclusions. He realized that the Marxist principle of complete equality of all men went against the grain of practical reality. It was an altogether fictitious stand. Compared to it, the Proutist principles appeared to have enshrined high ideals that were both logical and practical to implement. Attracted by its principles, Maheshwar Pandey was thoroughly convinced of their integrity and efficacy. He began to take an active part in all the open seminars organized to propagate the PROUT philosophy.

In spite of his convictions and willing participation in PROUT activities, Maheshwar Pandey adamantly refused to take initiation. Moulded in the atheistic tradition, his mind was trained in logical reasoning and analysis. Intuition and the subtleties of Brahma Sadhana were hitherto alien concepts. His rationally inclined mind could very well appreciate PROUT's logical concepts but revolted against the idea of intuitive spiritual practice. His arguments and deliberations could not stand the test of reason and logic, and each time he had to admit his defeat. Even so, his tainted mind was unable to shed the red stigma of religious antagonism. It was a concept too deeply rooted in his consciousness. He undoubtedly considered Baba to be a great intellectual and a philosopher beyond compare. He had no hesitation in acknowledging the extraordinary caliber of Baba's erudition and learning. But he could not bring himself to accept and acknowledge Baba's role as Sadguru.

His friend, who had introduced him to the Marga and to PROUT philosophy, would constantly urge and encourage him to take initiation. Ultimately, at the intellectual level he was prepared to admit that spiritual practice was one of the most vital aspects of human life, its fundamental need. In spite of this, he held himself back, refusing to be swayed and allured by the gift of *sadhana*. The ghost of "atheism," the hallmark of communist theory, was still entrenched in the inner recess of his mind. Some of these atheistic *samskaras*, sowed in the past, were preventing him from taking confident steps on the path of *dharma*. Very often he would give in to the persistent urges of his friend, and agree to take initiation. But at the very last minute he would bring up some excuse and evade the situation. This game of snakes and ladders continued and many opportunities slipped by. For a long while he carried on with this game of hide and seek.
